Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Alice has just learned addition. However, she hasn't learned the concept of "carrying" fully — instead of carrying to the next column, she carries to the column two columns to the left.

For example, the regular way to evaluate the sum $2039 + 2976$ would be as shown:

However, Alice evaluates it as shown:

In particular, this is what she does:

add $9$ and $6$ to make $15$, and carry the $1$ to the column two columns to the left, i. e. to the column "$0$ $9$";

add $3$ and $7$ to make $10$ and carry the $1$ to the column two columns to the left, i. e. to the column "$2$ $2$";

add $1$, $0$, and $9$ to make $10$ and carry the $1$ to the column two columns to the left, i. e. to the column above the plus sign;

add $1$, $2$ and $2$ to make $5$;

add $1$ to make $1$.

Thus, she ends up with the incorrect result of $15005$.

Alice comes up to Bob and says that she has added two numbers to get a result of $n$. However, Bob knows that Alice adds in her own way. Help Bob find the number of ordered pairs of positive integers such that when Alice adds them, she will get a result of $n$. Note that pairs $(a, b)$ and $(b, a)$ are considered different if $a \ne b$.


-----Input-----

The input consists of multiple test cases. The first line contains an integer $t$ ($1 \leq t \leq 1000$) — the number of test cases. The description of the test cases follows.

The only line of each test case contains an integer $n$ ($2 \leq n \leq 10^9$) — the number Alice shows Bob.


-----Output-----

For each test case, output one integer — the number of ordered pairs of positive integers such that when Alice adds them, she will get a result of $n$.


-----Examples-----

Input
5
100
12
8
2021
10000
Output
9
4
7
44
99

In the first test case, when Alice evaluates any of the sums $1 + 9$, $2 + 8$, $3 + 7$, $4 + 6$, $5 + 5$, $6 + 4$, $7 + 3$, $8 + 2$, or $9 + 1$, she will get a result of $100$. Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given an array $a$ of length $n$, and an integer $x$. You can perform the following operation as many times as you would like (possibly zero): replace two adjacent elements of the array by their sum. For example, if the initial array was $[3, 6, 9]$, in a single operation one can replace the last two elements by their sum, yielding an array $[3, 15]$, or replace the first two elements to get an array $[9, 9]$. Note that the size of the array decreases after each operation.

The beauty of an array $b=[b_1, \ldots, b_k]$ is defined as $\sum_{i=1}^k \left\lceil \frac{b_i}{x} \right\rceil$, which means that we divide each element by $x$, round it up to the nearest integer, and sum up the resulting values. For example, if $x = 3$, and the array is $[4, 11, 6]$, the beauty of the array is equal to $\left\lceil \frac{4}{3} \right\rceil + \left\lceil \frac{11}{3} \right\rceil + \left\lceil \frac{6}{3} \right\rceil = 2 + 4 + 2 = 8$.

Please determine the minimum and the maximum beauty you can get by performing some operations on the original array.


-----Input-----

The first input line contains a single integer $t$ — the number of test cases ($1 \le t \le 1000$).

The first line of each test case contains two integers $n$ and $x$ ($1 \leq n \leq 10^5$, $1 \leq x \leq 10^9$).

The next line contains $n$ integers $a_1, a_2, \ldots, a_n$ ($1 \leq a_i \leq 10^9$), the elements of the array $a$.

It is guaranteed that the sum of values of $n$ over all test cases does not exceed $10^5$.


-----Output-----

For each test case output two integers — the minimal and the maximal possible beauty.


-----Examples-----

Input
2
3 3
3 6 9
3 3
6 4 11
Output
6 6
7 8


-----Note-----

In the first test case the beauty of the array does not change if we perform any operations.

In the second example we can leave the array unchanged to attain the maximum beauty, and to get the minimum beauty one can replace two elements $4$ and $11$ with their sum, yielding an array $[6, 15]$, which has its beauty equal to $7$.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of length n and an integer x
2. We can replace any two adjacent elements with their sum (this reduces array size)
3. Beauty is defined as the sum of ceiling of each element divided by x
4. We need to find the minimum and maximum possible beauty

## Key Insights

### Maximum Beauty
- To maximize beauty, we want to keep elements separate so that each element contributes independently
- The maximum beauty is simply the sum of ceiling of each individual element divided by x
- This is achieved by doing zero operations (keeping all elements separate)

### Minimum Beauty
- To minimize beauty, we want to combine elements strategically
- When we combine two elements, their total contribution becomes ceiling(total_sum / x)
- The key insight is that ceiling(a/x) + ceiling(b/x) ≥ ceiling((a+b)/x) 
- This is due to the property of ceiling functions: ceiling(a/x) + ceiling(b/x) ≥ ceiling((a+b)/x)
- Equality holds when a % x and b % x don't cause a "carry-over" that would increase the ceiling value
- To minimize, we want to combine elements in a way that minimizes the total ceiling sum
- The minimum beauty is achieved when we combine all elements into one (the sum of all elements) and take ceiling(sum/x)

But wait - is combining all elements always optimal? 

Let me think about this more carefully. 

Actually, the minimum beauty might not always be ceiling(total_sum/x). Consider when some individual elements already have a remainder that would cause a carry-over.

The key insight is that we can use a greedy approach:
- The minimum beauty is at least ceiling(total_sum/x)
- We need to check if we can achieve exactly ceiling(total_sum/x)

However, there's a subtlety with the ceiling function. The important property is that ceiling(a/x) + ceiling(b/x) ≥ ceiling((a+b)/x), with equality when (a % x + b % x) < x.

This suggests that we can combine adjacent elements optimally, and the minimum beauty is indeed ceiling(sum_of_all_elements / x).

But let's verify with the examples:

Example 1: [3,6,9], x=3
- Individual ceils: 1+2+3 = 6
- Combined: ceiling(18/3) = 6
- So min = max = 6 ✓

Example 2: [6,4,11], x=3
- Individual ceils: 2+2+4 = 8 (ceiling(6/3)=2, ceiling(4/3)=2, ceiling(11/3)=4)
- Combined: ceiling(21/3) = 7
- Max = 8, min = 7 ✓

Wait, this matches the expected output. So the pattern is:
- Maximum beauty = sum of ceiling(a_i/x) for all i
- Minimum beauty = ceiling(sum(a_i)/x)

But is the minimum beauty always ceiling(sum/x)? 

Yes, because:
1. Each operation can only decrease or maintain the beauty (due to ceiling(a/x) + ceiling(b/x) ≥ ceiling((a+b)/x))
2. The total sum remains constant
3. So we can always achieve beauty = ceiling(total_sum/x)
4. And we can never go below this value

Therefore:
- Maximum beauty = ∑ ceil(a_i/x)
- Minimum beauty = ceil(∑ a_i / x)

## Implementation

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Maximum beauty: sum of ceiling of each element divided by x
    max_beauty = 0
    for val in a:
        max_beauty += math.ceil(val / x)
    
    # Minimum beauty: ceiling of total sum divided by x
    total_sum = sum(a)
    min_beauty = math.ceil(total_sum / x)
    
    return min_beauty, max_beauty

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    min_b, max_b = solve()
    print(min_b, max_b)
```

Wait, let me verify the condition about ceiling properties more carefully.

The key property is: ceiling(a/x) + ceiling(b/x) ≥ ceiling((a+b)/x)

This means that combining adjacent elements never decreases the total beauty, and sometimes increases it. Therefore:
- Maximum beauty is achieved when we don't combine any elements (all separate)
- Minimum beauty is achieved when we combine all elements (sum them up)

So yes, my reasoning is correct.

```python
import math

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Maximum beauty: sum of ceiling of each element divided by x
    max_beauty = sum(math.ceil(val / x) for val in a)
    
    # Minimum beauty: ceiling of total sum divided by x
    total_sum = sum(a)
    min_beauty = math.ceil(total_sum / x)
    
    print(min_beauty, max_beauty)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of length n and an integer x
2. We can replace any two adjacent elements with their sum (reducing array size by 1 each time)
3. Beauty = sum of ceiling of each element divided by x
4. We need to find minimum and maximum possible beauty

## Key Insights

### Maximum Beauty
- To maximize beauty, we want to keep elements as separate as possible
- The maximum beauty is achieved when we don't perform any operations (keep original array)
- This is because ceiling function is non-decreasing, and splitting doesn't help us increase the sum
- So maximum beauty = sum of ceil(a_i / x) for all i

### Minimum Beauty
- To minimize beauty, we want to combine elements optimally
- When we combine two adjacent elements, we replace them with their sum
- The key insight is that ceiling function has the property: ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x)
- This means merging two elements can only decrease or keep the same beauty
- However, we need to find the optimal way to merge

Wait, let me reconsider the minimum beauty.

Actually, the key insight is that we can merge any two adjacent elements, and we want to minimize the total ceiling sum. Since ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x), merging reduces the sum.

To minimize the beauty, we should merge elements to get the smallest possible sum of ceilings.

But we can merge any adjacent elements in any order. The important observation is that we can merge any two elements that are adjacent, and eventually we can merge the entire array into a single element.

However, we need to check if that's always optimal.

Actually, let me think about when merging helps:
- If we have two numbers a and b, then ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x)
- Equality holds when a mod x and b mod x don't cause an overflow
- So merging always reduces or keeps the same beauty

Therefore, the minimum beauty should be ceil(total_sum / x), because we can merge all elements into one.

But is this always achievable? Yes, we can merge adjacent elements in any order to eventually get the entire array sum as one element.

However, we need to verify this with the examples.

Example 1: [3,6,9], x=3
- Original beauty: ceil(3/3) + ceil(6/3) + ceil(9/3) = 1+2+3 = 6
- Total sum = 18, ceil(18/3) = 6
- Answer is 6 6 - matches

Example 2: [6,4,11], x=3
- Original beauty: ceil(6/3) + ceil(4/3) + ceil(11/3) = 2 + 2 + 4 = 8
- Total sum = 21, ceil(21/3) = 7  
- Answer is 7 8 - matches

So the pattern is:
- Maximum beauty = sum of ceil(a_i / x) for all i
- Minimum beauty = ceil(total_sum / x)

This makes sense because:
1. Maximum is achieved by keeping all elements separate (no merging)
2. Minimum is achieved by merging all elements into one (since ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x))

## Step-by-step Solution

1. For each test case:
   - Calculate maximum beauty by summing ceil(a_i / x) for each element
   - Calculate minimum beauty by calculating ceil(total_sum / x)
2. Output both values

## Implementation

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Maximum beauty: keep all elements separate
    max_beauty = 0
    for num in a:
        max_beauty += math.ceil(num / x)
    
    # Minimum beauty: merge all elements into one
    total_sum = sum(a)
    min_beauty = math.ceil(total_sum / x)
    
    return max_beauty, min_beauty

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    max_b, min_b = solve()
    print(max_b, min_b)
```

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: [3,6,9], x=3
- max_beauty = ceil(3/3) + ceil(6/3) + ceil(9/3) = 1 + 2 + 3 = 6
- total_sum = 18, min_beauty = ceil(18/3) = 6
- Output: 6 6 ✓

Example 2: [6,4,11], x=3  
- max_beauty = ceil(6/3) + ceil(4/3) + ceil(11/3) = 2 + 2 + 4 = 8
- total_sum = 21, min_beauty = ceil(21/3) = 7
- Output: 7 8 ✓

The solution is correct!

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given an array $a$ of length $n$, and an integer $x$. You can perform the following operation as many times as you would like (possibly zero): replace two adjacent elements of the array by their sum. For example, if the initial array was $[3, 6, 9]$, in a single operation one can replace the last two elements by their sum, yielding an array $[3, 15]$, or replace the first two elements to get an array $[9, 9]$. Note that the size of the array decreases after each operation.

The beauty of an array $b=[b_1, \ldots, b_k]$ is defined as $\sum_{i=1}^k \left\lceil \frac{b_i}{x} \right\rceil$, which means that we divide each element by $x$, round it up to the nearest integer, and sum up the resulting values. For example, if $x = 3$, and the array is $[4, 11, 6]$, the beauty of the array is equal to $\left\lceil \frac{4}{3} \right\rceil + \left\lceil \frac{11}{3} \right\rceil + \left\lceil \frac{6}{3} \right\rceil = 2 + 4 + 2 = 8$.

Please determine the minimum and the maximum beauty you can get by performing some operations on the original array.


-----Input-----

The first input line contains a single integer $t$ — the number of test cases ($1 \le t \le 1000$).

The first line of each test case contains two integers $n$ and $x$ ($1 \leq n \leq 10^5$, $1 \leq x \leq 10^9$).

The next line contains $n$ integers $a_1, a_2, \ldots, a_n$ ($1 \leq a_i \leq 10^9$), the elements of the array $a$.

It is guaranteed that the sum of values of $n$ over all test cases does not exceed $10^5$.


-----Output-----

For each test case output two integers — the minimal and the maximal possible beauty.


-----Examples-----

Input

3 3
3 6 9
3 3
6 4 11
Output

7 8

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of length n and an integer x
2. We can replace any two adjacent elements with their sum (this reduces array size)
3. Beauty is defined as sum of ceil(element/x) for all elements
4. We need to find minimum and maximum possible beauty

## Key Insights

### Maximum Beauty
- To maximize beauty, we want to keep all elements separate
- This means we never merge any elements
- Maximum beauty = sum of ceil(ai/x) for all i

### Minimum Beauty
- To minimize beauty, we want to merge elements strategically
- When we merge two adjacent elements, we replace ceil(a_i/x) + ceil(a_j/x) with ceil((a_i+a_j)/x)
- We need to determine when merging reduces the total beauty

Important property: For any two numbers a and b, ceil((a+b)/x) ≤ ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x)

This is because:
- (a+b)/x ≤ a/x + b/x
- ceil((a+b)/x) ≤ ceil(a/x + b/x) ≤ ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x)

Equality holds when a/x and b/x don't have fractional parts that sum to at least 1.

However, we can achieve the minimum beauty by merging optimally. The key insight is that we can always merge adjacent elements until we get one element (the sum of all elements).

But we need to be careful - merging doesn't always help. Let's think about when merging reduces beauty.

Actually, let's think differently. The minimum beauty occurs when we merge optimally. 

Consider: We can merge any adjacent elements. The key observation is that we can achieve the minimum beauty by considering the "wasted" parts.

Wait, let me reconsider with the examples:

Example 1: [3,6,9], x=3
- Beauty of [3,6,9]: ceil(3/3)+ceil(6/3)+ceil(9/3) = 1+2+3 = 6
- If we merge 6 and 9: get [3,15], beauty = ceil(3/3)+ceil(15/3) = 1+5 = 6
- If we merge 3 and 6: get [9,9], beauty = ceil(9/3)+ceil(9/3) = 3+3 = 6
- If we merge 3 and 6 then 9: get [15], beauty = ceil(15/3) = 5? Wait, that's not right.

Wait, I miscalculated: ceil(15/3) = 5, so [15] has beauty 5, which is less than 6.

But the expected output is 6 6, meaning minimum is 6.

This suggests my analysis is wrong.

Let me recalculate example 1:
- [3,6,9]: ceil(3/3)=1, ceil(6/3)=2, ceil(9/3)=3 → sum = 6
- [3,15]: ceil(3/3)=1, ceil(15/3)=5 → sum = 6
- [9,9]: ceil(9/3)=3, ceil(9/3)=3 → sum = 6
- [24]: ceil(24/3)=8 → sum = 8

Wait, this suggests the minimum should be 6, not 8.

Actually, let me check [3+6+9=24]: ceil(24/3)=8.

So all possible configurations give at least 6? 

But [3,15] gives 1+5=6, and [9,9] gives 3+3=6. So minimum is 6.

For example 2: [6,4,11], x=3
- Original: ceil(6/3)=2, ceil(4/3)=2, ceil(11/3)=4 → sum = 8
- Merge 6 and 4: [10,11]: ceil(10/3)=4, ceil(11/3)=4 → sum = 8
- Merge 4 and 11: [6,15]: ceil(6/3)=2, ceil(15/3)=5 → sum = 7
- Merge all: [21]: ceil(21/3)=7

So minimum is 7, maximum is 8.

The pattern seems to be:
- Maximum beauty = sum of ceil(ai/x)
- Minimum beauty = ceil(sum of all ai / x)

Wait, no - in example 1, sum of all ai / x = 18/3 = 6, ceil(6) = 6. This matches.

In example 2, sum of all ai = 21, 21/3 = 7, ceil(7) = 7. This matches.

Is it always true that minimum beauty = ceil(sum(a_i) / x)?

Yes! Because:
- We can always merge all elements into one and get beauty = ceil(total_sum / x)
- And ceil(sum(ai)/x) ≤ ceil(ai/x) + ceil(a_j/x) for any adjacent merge, so we can't do better than this
- So the minimum beauty is exactly ceil(total_sum / x)

Therefore:
- Maximum beauty = sum of ceil(ai/x) 
- Minimum beauty = ceil(sum(ai)/x)

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: 
- Max: ceil(3/3)+ceil(6/3)+ceil(9/3) = 1+2+3 = 6
- Min: ceil((3+6+9)/3) = ceil(18/3) = 6
- Result: 6 6 ✓

Example 2:
- Max: ceil(6/3)+ceil(4/3)+ceil(11/3) = 2+2+4 = 8  
- Min: ceil((6+4+11)/3) = ceil(21/3) = 7
- Result: 7 8 ✓

Perfect!

## Solution

```python
import math

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Maximum beauty: sum of ceil(ai/x)
    max_beauty = 0
    for num in a:
        max_beauty += (num + x - 1) // x  # This is equivalent to ceil(num/x)
    
    # Minimum beauty: ceil(sum(ai)/x)
    total_sum = sum(a)
    min_beauty = (total_sum + x - 1) // x  # This is equivalent to ceil(total_sum/x)
    
    print(min_beauty, max_beauty)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of length n and an integer x
2. We can replace any two adjacent elements with their sum (reducing array size by 1 each time)
3. Beauty = sum of ⌈element/x⌉ for all elements
4. We need to find minimum and maximum possible beauty

## Key Insights

### Maximum Beauty
- To maximize beauty, we want to keep elements as separate as possible
- The maximum beauty is achieved when we don't perform any operations (original array)
- This is because ⌈a/x⌉ + ⌈b/x⌉ ≥ ⌈(a+b)/x⌉ in most cases
- We can prove that ⌈a/x⌉ + ⌈b/x⌉ ≥ ⌈(a+b)/x⌉ (this is a known inequality)
- Therefore, maximum beauty = sum of ⌈a_i/x⌉ for all i

### Minimum Beauty
- To minimize beauty, we want to combine elements optimally
- The key insight is that we can combine adjacent elements in any order
- The minimum beauty is achieved by combining all elements into a single element (the total sum)
- This gives us ⌈(sum of all elements)/x⌉
- However, we need to be careful - sometimes combining different elements might give better results

Let me think deeper about the minimum beauty:

Actually, we need to consider if combining elements can produce a smaller beauty. Let's analyze the inequality:

⌈a/x⌉ + ⌈b/x⌉ ≥ ⌈(a+b)/x⌉

This inequality holds true, with equality when a mod x + b mod x < x.

Therefore, whenever we combine two elements, the beauty doesn't increase, and might decrease.

So the minimum beauty should be ⌈(sum of all elements)/x⌉.

Wait, let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: [3,6,9], x=3
- Original: ⌈3/3⌉ + ⌈6/3⌉ + ⌈9/3⌉ = 1+2+3 = 6
- Combined: ⌈(3+6+9)/3⌉ = ⌈18/3⌉ = 6
- So min = max = 6 ✓

Example 2: [6,4,11], x=3
- Original: ⌈6/3⌉ + ⌈4/3⌉ + ⌈11/3⌉ = 2+2+4 = 8
- Combined: ⌈(6+4+11)/3⌉ = ⌈21/3⌉ = 7
- So min = 7, max = 8 ✓

Perfect! This matches the expected output.

## Algorithm

1. For maximum beauty: sum of ⌈a_i/x⌉ for all elements
2. For minimum beauty: ⌈(sum of all elements)/x⌉

## Implementation

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Maximum beauty: sum of ceil(a_i/x)
    max_beauty = 0
    for num in a:
        max_beauty += math.ceil(num / x)
    
    # Minimum beauty: ceil(sum of all elements / x)
    total_sum = sum(a)
    min_beauty = math.ceil(total_sum / x)
    
    return min_beauty, max_beauty

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    min_val, max_val = solve()
    print(min_val, max_val)
```

The solution is elegant:

- Maximum beauty: We never want to combine elements since combining always reduces or maintains beauty (due to the ceiling inequality)
- Minimum beauty: We want to combine all elements into one, since that gives us the smallest possible value in the sum of ceilings

The time complexity is O(n) per test case, which is efficient given the constraints.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have an array and can replace any two adjacent elements with their sum. We need to find the minimum and maximum possible beauty, where beauty is defined as the sum of ceiling of each element divided by x.

Key observations:
- When we replace two adjacent elements `a[i]` and `a[i+1]` with `a[i] + a[i+1]`, the beauty changes from `⌈a[i]/x⌉ + ⌈a[i+1]/x⌉` to `⌈(a[i] + a[i+1])/x⌉`.
- The ceiling function has the property that `⌈a/x⌉ + ⌈b/x⌉ ≥ ⌈(a+b)/x⌉`, with equality when `a mod x + b mod x < x`.
- When `a mod x + b mod x ≥ x`, we have `⌈a/x⌉ + ⌈b/x⌉ = ⌈(a+b)/x⌉ + 1`.

## Key Insights

### Maximum Beauty
- To maximize beauty, we want to minimize the number of "overflows" where we get an extra ceiling.
- The maximum beauty occurs when we don't perform any operations - we keep the original array.
- This is because merging adjacent elements can only reduce or maintain beauty (never increase it).

### Minimum Beauty
- To minimize beauty, we want to maximize the number of "overflows" where we get an extra ceiling.
- The minimum beauty occurs when we merge adjacent elements in a way that maximizes the number of times `a mod x + b mod x ≥ x`.
- We can think of this as having a "carry" system - when the sum of remainders when divided by x is at least x, we get an extra ceiling.

Actually, let me reconsider the minimum beauty:

When we merge two elements, the beauty changes from `⌈a/x⌉ + ⌈b/x⌉` to `⌈(a+b)/x⌉`.

Since `⌈a/x⌉ + ⌈b/x⌉ ≥ ⌈(a+b)/x⌉`, merging can only reduce or maintain the beauty.

But the ceiling function has the property: `⌈a/x⌉ + ⌈b/x⌉ - ⌈(a+b)/x⌉` can be 0 or 1.

This difference is 1 if and only if `(a mod x) + (b mod x) ≥ x`.

So to minimize beauty, we want to create as many such "overflow" situations as possible.

The minimum beauty is achieved when we merge adjacent elements that have remainders summing to at least x.

Actually, there's a better approach:

The minimum beauty is the ceiling of the total sum divided by x, plus any "carry" that happens from merging elements with large remainders.

Wait, let me think differently:

The minimum beauty is actually `⌈sum(a) / x⌉`, because no matter how we merge, the total sum remains the same, and the ceiling of the total sum divided by x is a lower bound.

For maximum beauty, it's when we don't merge anything, which gives us the sum of ceilings of individual elements.

So:
- Maximum beauty = Σ⌈a[i]/x⌉
- Minimum beauty = ⌈(Σa[i])/x⌉

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: [3,6,9], x=3
- Sum = 18, ⌈18/3⌉ = 6
- Max beauty = ⌈3/3⌉ + ⌈6/3⌉ + ⌈9/3⌉ = 1 + 2 + 3 = 6
- Min beauty = 6
- Output: 6 6 ✓

Example 2: [6,4,11], x=3  
- Sum = 21, ⌈21/3⌉ = 7
- Max beauty = ⌈6/3⌉ + ⌈4/3⌉ + ⌈11/3⌉ = 2 + 2 + 4 = 8
- Min beauty = 7
- Output: 7 8 ✓

This matches perfectly!

## Algorithm

For each test case:
1. Calculate the sum of all elements
2. Minimum beauty = ⌈sum / x⌉
3. Maximum beauty = sum of ⌈a[i]/x⌉ for all elements

```python
import math

def solve():
    t = int(input())
    for _ in range(t):
        n, x = map(int, input().split())
        a = list(map(int, input().split()))
        
        # Maximum beauty: sum of ceiling of each element divided by x
        max_beauty = 0
        for num in a:
            max_beauty += math.ceil(num / x)
        
        # Minimum beauty: ceiling of total sum divided by x
        total_sum = sum(a)
        min_beauty = math.ceil(total_sum / x)
        
        print(min_beauty, max_beauty)

solve()
```

Note that the size of the array decreases after each operation.

The beauty of an array $b=[b_1, \ldots, b_k]$ is defined as $\sum_{i=1}^k \left\lceil \frac{b_i}{x} \right\rceil$, which means that we divide each element by $x$, round it up to the nearest integer, and sum up the resulting values. For example, if $x = 3$, and the array is $[4, 11, 6]$, the beauty of the array is equal to $\left\lceil \frac{4}{3} \right\rceil + \left\lceil \frac{11}{3} \right\rceil + \left\lceil \frac{6}{3} \right\rceil = 2 + 4 + 2 = 8$.

Please determine the minimum and the maximum beauty you can get by performing some operations on the original array.


-----Input-----

The first input line contains a single integer $t$ — the number of test cases ($1 \le t \le 1000$).

The first line of each test case contains two integers $n$ and $x$ ($1 \leq n \leq 10^5$, $1 \leq x \leq 10^9$).

The next line contains $n$ integers $a_1, a_2, \ldots, a_n$ ($1 \leq a_i \leq 10^9$), the elements of the array $a$.

It is guaranteed that the sum of values of $n$ over all test cases does not exceed $10^5$.


-----Output-----

For each test case output two integers — the minimal and the maximal possible beauty.


-----Examples-----

Input
2
3 3
3 6 9
3 3
6 4 11
Output
6 6
7 8


-----Note-----

In the first test case the beauty of the array does not change if we perform any operations.

In the second example we can leave the array unchanged to attain the maximum beauty, and to get the minimum beauty one can replace two elements $4$ and $11$ with their sum, yielding an array $[6, 15]$, which has its beauty equal to $7$.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of length n and an integer x
2. We can replace two adjacent elements with their sum (reducing array size)
3. Beauty is defined as sum of ceiling of each element divided by x
4. We need to find minimum and maximum possible beauty

## Key Insights

### Maximum Beauty
- To maximize beauty, we want to minimize grouping of elements
- The maximum beauty occurs when we don't perform any operations (keep array as is)
- This is because ceiling(a/x) + ceiling(b/x) ≥ ceiling((a+b)/x) in general
- Actually, let me verify: ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x)
- This inequality holds because ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ (a/x) + (b/x) = (a+b)/x, and ceiling function is at least the value
- But ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x) is true
- So maximum beauty is achieved when we don't merge any elements

### Minimum Beauty
- To minimize beauty, we want to merge elements strategically
- When we merge two elements a and b into a+b, the beauty changes from ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) to ceil((a+b)/x)
- We need to find if ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x)
- This is always true, so merging reduces beauty only in specific cases
- Actually, ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x) is a known mathematical property
- The beauty after merging is always less than or equal to the beauty before merging
- So we want to merge optimally to minimize the total

Wait, let me reconsider the minimum beauty.

Actually, when we merge two elements a and b into a+b:
- Original beauty contribution: ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x)
- New beauty contribution: ceil((a+b)/x)

Since ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x) by properties of ceiling function, merging always reduces or maintains the beauty.

Therefore, the minimum beauty is achieved when we merge optimally - we want to make as many large groups as possible.

But how do we find the optimal merging? 

Actually, I think the key insight is:
- Maximum beauty = sum of ceil(a_i/x) for all i (no merging)
- Minimum beauty: we can merge elements optimally, and the minimum beauty equals ceil(sum(a)/x)

Why? Because the total sum is preserved, and we can merge all elements into one, giving ceiling(total_sum/x).

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: [3,6,9], x=3
- Total sum = 18, ceil(18/3) = 6
- Sum of individual ceil(a_i/x): ceil(3/3) + ceil(6/3) + ceil(9/3) = 1 + 2 + 3 = 6
- Both are 6 → matches

Example 2: [6,4,11], x=3  
- Total sum = 21, ceil(21/3) = 7
- Sum of individual ceil(a_i/x): ceil(6/3) + ceil(4/3) + ceil(11/3) = 2 + 2 + 4 = 8
- So min = 7, max = 8 → matches

Yes! This works.

## Algorithm

1. Maximum beauty = sum of ceil(a_i / x) for all elements
2. Minimum beauty = ceil(total_sum / x)

## Implementation

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Maximum beauty: no merging, each element separately
    max_beauty = 0
    for val in a:
        max_beauty += math.ceil(val / x)
    
    # Minimum beauty: merge all elements into one
    total_sum = sum(a)
    min_beauty = math.ceil(total_sum / x)
    
    return min_beauty, max_beauty

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    min_b, max_b = solve()
    print(min_b, max_b)
```

Let me double-check the mathematical property:
- For any a, b: ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x)
- This is true because ceil(a/x) ≥ a/x and ceil(b/x) ≥ b/x, so sum ≥ (a+b)/x
- And since ceiling is integer, if ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) > ceil((a+b)/x), it would contradict the property
- Actually, this inequality holds, so merging always reduces or maintains beauty

Therefore, minimum beauty is ceil(sum(a)/x) and maximum is sum(ceil(a_i/x)).

The solution is correct.

Note that the size of the array decreases after each operation.

For example, if $x = 3$, and the array is $[4, 11, 6]$, the beauty of the array is equal to $\left\lceil \frac{4}{3} \right\rceil + \left\lceil \frac{11}{3} \right\rceil + \left\lceil \frac{6}{3} \right\rceil = 2 + 4 + 2 = 8$.

The next line contains $n$ integers $a_1, a_2, \ldots, a_n$ ($1 \leq a_i \leq 10^9$), the elements of the array $a$.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of length n and an integer x
2. We can replace any two adjacent elements with their sum (reducing array length by 1 each time)
3. Beauty = sum of ceil(value/x) for all elements in the array
4. We need to find minimum and maximum possible beauty

## Key Insights

### Maximum Beauty
- To maximize beauty, we want to keep elements as separate as possible
- The maximum beauty is achieved when we don't perform any operations at all (keep all original elements)
- This is because ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≤ ceil((a+b)/x) in general, but sometimes equality holds
- Actually, let me reconsider: ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x) due to ceiling function properties
- So the maximum beauty would be when we have all original elements separate

Wait, let me think more carefully about this:

For any two positive numbers a and b:
- ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x)

This is because ceil(a/x) ≥ a/x and ceil(b/x) ≥ b/x, so their sum ≥ (a+b)/x, and since ceiling function gives the smallest integer ≥ value, the sum of ceilings is at least the ceiling of the sum.

Therefore, **maximum beauty = sum of ceil(a_i/x) for all i** (no operations)

### Minimum Beauty
- To minimize beauty, we want to combine elements to reduce the sum of ceilings
- When we combine two adjacent elements a and b, we get ceil((a+b)/x) instead of ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x)
- Since ceil((a+b)/x) ≤ ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) is not always true, we need to think differently
- Actually, we have: ceil((a+b)/x) ≤ ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) is true because (a+b)/x ≤ ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) since both ceil(a/x) ≥ a/x and ceil(b/x) ≥ b/x

But we want to minimize the total sum. The key insight is that we can combine elements in any order, and we want to minimize the final sum.

The minimum beauty occurs when we combine all elements into a single element (the sum of all elements), because:
- Beauty = ceil(total_sum / x)

Is this always optimal? Let me verify:
- We get ceil((sum of all elements)/x)
- This might be less than the sum of individual ceilings

Actually, it's not necessarily true that combining all elements gives the minimum. Let me reconsider.

The critical insight is that the beauty function is convex in nature. When we combine two elements a and b, the change in beauty is:
- Δ = ceil((a+b)/x) - (ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x))

This Δ is always negative or zero. Since ceil((a+b)/x) ≤ ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x), combining always reduces or maintains the beauty.

Wait, let me check with an example:
- a=1, b=1, x=2: ceil(1/2)+ceil(1/2) = 1+1 = 2, ceil((1+1)/2) = ceil(2/2) = 1
- So we gain -1

This means combining always reduces beauty (unless equality holds).

Therefore:
- **Minimum beauty** = ceil(total_sum / x) (all elements combined into one)
- **Maximum beauty** = sum of ceil(a_i / x) for all i (no operations)

## Verification with Examples

Example 1: [3,6,9], x=3
- Total sum = 18, ceil(18/3) = ceil(6) = 6
- Sum of individual ceilings: ceil(3/3) + ceil(6/3) + ceil(9/3) = 1 + 2 + 3 = 6
- Result: 6 6 ✓

Example 2: [6,4,11], x=3  
- Total sum = 21, ceil(21/3) = ceil(7) = 7
- Sum of individual ceilings: ceil(6/3) + ceil(4/3) + ceil(11/3) = 2 + 2 + 4 = 8
- Result: 7 8 ✓

Perfect! The solution works.

## Implementation

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Maximum beauty: sum of ceil(a_i / x) for all i
    max_beauty = 0
    for val in a:
        max_beauty += math.ceil(val / x)
    
    # Minimum beauty: ceil(total_sum / x)
    total_sum = sum(a)
    min_beauty = math.ceil(total_sum / x)
    
    return min_beauty, max_beauty

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    min_val, max_val = solve()
    print(min_val, max_val)
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of length n and an integer x
2. We can replace two adjacent elements with their sum any number of times
3. The beauty of an array is the sum of ceil(element/x) for each element
4. We need to find the minimum and maximum possible beauty

## Key Insights

Let me think about what happens when we merge two adjacent elements:

- If we merge elements a and b, we replace them with (a+b)
- The beauty changes from ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) to ceil((a+b)/x)

The key insight is that ceil((a+b)/x) ≤ ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) - 1 in most cases, but there are cases where it might be equal.

Actually, let's think about the relationship:
- ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x)
- This is because ceil(a/x) ≥ a/x and ceil(b/x) ≥ b/x, so their sum ≥ (a+b)/x
- Since ceil((a+b)/x) is the smallest integer ≥ (a+b)/x, we have ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x)

So merging elements can only decrease or keep the same beauty, never increase it.

Wait, that's not quite right. Let me reconsider:

- ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x) is always true
- This means merging two elements can only decrease or keep the same beauty
- Therefore, the minimum beauty is achieved when we merge everything into a single element

But for maximum beauty, we want to avoid merging when it reduces beauty. Since merging never increases beauty, the maximum beauty is achieved when we don't merge anything at all (keep the original array).

Wait, let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: [3,6,9], x=3
- Original: ceil(3/3) + ceil(6/3) + ceil(9/3) = 1 + 2 + 3 = 6
- If we merge 3 and 6: [9,9], beauty = ceil(9/3) + ceil(9/3) = 3 + 3 = 6
- If we merge 6 and 9: [3,15], beauty = ceil(3/3) + ceil(15/3) = 1 + 5 = 6
- If we merge all: [18], beauty = ceil(18/3) = 6

So all give beauty 6.

Example 2: [6,4,11], x=3
- Original: ceil(6/3) + ceil(4/3) + ceil(11/3) = 2 + 2 + 4 = 8
- If we merge 4 and 11: [6,15], beauty = ceil(6/3) + ceil(15/3) = 2 + 5 = 7
- If we merge 6 and 4: [10,11], beauty = ceil(10/3) + ceil(11/3) = 4 + 4 = 8

So maximum is 8 (original), minimum is 7 (merged 4,11).

## Key Insight

1. **Maximum beauty**: This is achieved when we don't merge anything at all. The beauty is just sum(ceil(a_i/x)) for all i.

2. **Minimum beauty**: This is achieved when we merge everything into a single element. The beauty is ceil(total_sum/x).

Wait, is that always optimal? Let me think if there's a better way to merge.

Actually, the total sum of all elements remains constant regardless of merging operations. So the final sum after any number of operations is always sum(a_i).

Since ceil(total_sum/x) is achieved when we have one element, and merging only reduces or keeps the same beauty (because ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x)), the minimum beauty is indeed ceil(sum(a_i)/x).

But wait - is it possible that merging in a different order could give even less beauty? No, because the inequality ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x) holds for all positive a,b,x.

Therefore:
- Maximum beauty = sum(ceil(a_i/x)) for all i
- Minimum beauty = ceil(sum(a_i)/x)

Let me verify with example 2:
- Max: ceil(6/3) + ceil(4/3) + ceil(11/3) = 2 + 2 + 4 = 8 ✓
- Min: ceil((6+4+11)/3) = ceil(21/3) = 7 ✓

Perfect!

## Solution

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Maximum beauty: sum of ceil(a_i / x) for each element
    max_beauty = 0
    for val in a:
        max_beauty += math.ceil(val / x)
    
    # Minimum beauty: ceil(total_sum / x)
    total_sum = sum(a)
    min_beauty = math.ceil(total_sum / x)
    
    return min_beauty, max_beauty

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    min_b, max_b = solve()
    print(min_b, max_b)
```
